- **It was never only the two permissions on the onboarding panel.** Those 24 lines cover four services — Accessibility 10, Screen Recording 6, Microphone 4, Desktop folder 4 — and carry five distinct hashes. One appears in all 24 (the running build); the other four appear exactly as often as their own service, meaning each permission was pinned to a different, long-gone build. Voice commands and Desktop file access had been failing the same way, unnoticed, because nothing surfaces them the way the onboarding panel surfaces the other two.
- **Worktrees change the binary even when the source is identical.** The built executable embeds exactly one absolute path — its own `MacAgent_MacAgent.bundle` inside its own worktree. Same source, different worktree, different bytes, different hash.
- **The cdhash is path-independent, which kills the obvious cheap fix.** Copying a signed bundle elsewhere leaves its hash unchanged, so packaging to one stable path does nothing for grant survival. That option was measured and then dropped by founder decision rather than argued about.
- **A repackage with no source change is deterministic.** Two consecutive `package-app.sh` runs produced the identical `41bdd194…`. Only a code change breaks the grant — which is every rebuild that matters.
- **`security find-identity -v` cannot see a locally created identity.** `-v` filters to certificates with a trusted chain; a self-signed one has none, so `-v -p codesigning` reports it absent while `-p codesigning` lists it as `(CSSMERR_TP_NOT_TRUSTED)` — and codesign signs with it regardless. Detection code that uses `-v` will conclude the identity is missing forever. `sonny_signing_identity_present` deliberately omits it.
- **macOS's Security framework cannot read OpenSSL 3's default PKCS#12 encoding.** `security import` fails with "MAC verification failed during PKCS12 import (wrong password?)", which reads like a passphrase bug and is not one. `-legacy -keypbe PBE-SHA1-3DES -certpbe PBE-SHA1-3DES -macalg sha1` is required.
- **Neither `-A` nor `-T /usr/bin/codesign` on import prevents the key-access dialog** — the modern partition list overrides both. Until codesign is authorised, every build blocks on a GUI dialog. This presented as `package-app.sh` hanging with no output whatsoever, which is why the setup script now raises that dialog on purpose and `package-app.sh` prints a line explaining a stall before it can happen. The alternative, `security set-key-partition-list`, needs the login keychain password, and a script that asks for that is worse than a dialog macOS asks for itself.
- **An ad-hoc requirement prints commented out and a certificate's does not** — `# designated => cdhash …` versus `designated => identifier …`. A `grep '^designated'` silently drops exactly the case the warning exists for. Caught by testing the ad-hoc branch rather than only the happy path.
- **`com.apple.security.get-task-allow` will block notarization later.** SwiftPM generates that entitlement for debug builds and `package-app.sh` passes it through. It is correct today and harmless with a local certificate, but Apple rejects notarization of anything carrying it. Filed as its own ticket rather than fixed here — notarization is SONNY-106 section E's work, not this ticket's.

Known limitations / deferred scope:
- **This is not the v1 release requirement and must not be recorded as satisfying it.** SONNY-106 section E requires a Developer ID signed *and notarized* build; section F tracks the Apple Developer enrolment that gates it (started 2026-08-17, not complete). A self-signed local certificate does nothing for anyone else installing Sonny — Gatekeeper still refuses these builds on any other Mac. Stated at the founder's explicit instruction, in the config file, both scripts, `CLAUDE.md` and the manual-test checklist, precisely so a later reader cannot tick the condition off the back of this work.
- **End-to-end grant persistence is confirmed only in mechanism, not in the live app.** What is measured is that the designated requirement no longer moves when the build does — including a deliberate binary change that shifted the hash while the requirement stayed byte-identical. That a real TCC grant then survives a rebuild is the founder's manual-test item, because no agent can drive the app.
- The in-app "your grant belongs to a different build" notice was declined (see below); the same information went into the manual-test checklist's troubleshooting instead.

Open questions (required, write "none" if true): none. All four options in SONNY-153's description were put to the founder and decided on 2026-08-17; the decisions and their reasoning are on the ticket. Two are worth restating here because they are product-level rather than tooling-level. First, **the 2026-08-14 no-explanatory-copy rule covers how-it-works explanations, not only data-sent-to-AI copy** — explaining code signing in the product is squarely the former, and the narrower reading of that rule is "fair on the letter and wrong on the intent" (founder, 2026-08-17). Second, **the venue was wrong rather than the need** — the information helps a developer, not a user, so it belongs in the manual-test documentation where the people who hit it will look.

Next branch: unchanged by this. The locked roadmap's next rows (D, E, J, 12) are unaffected; this branch only removes an obstacle in front of every one of their manual passes.
### Branch: feature/terminal-screen-check
Status: complete
Date: 2026-08-17
Tickets: SONNY-139 (Sonny refuses to control a window that is showing a shell — the runtime half of SONNY-102's chosen approach, layered after the static deny list and never replacing it)
Reviewed by: fresh session (per WORKFLOW.md step 7), three cycles, same reviewer throughout. Cycle 1 at `9cacfc0`: five findings, **all fixed**. Cycle 3 re-check at `d21dc38`: all five verified fixed on evidence, plus two new findings (N1 independence, N2 coverage), **both fixed** — the cap bounds rounds of review, not the fix-in-branch rule. The coordinator adjudicated both rounds and verifies the cycle-3 fixes directly; there is no fourth reviewer session.

Spec sections covered: §7.4 (the terminal trust boundary — the categorical rule this implements more of), §13 (the screen-control loop this refuses inside), §12.3 (the redaction pass whose OCR output is the signal). Nothing new; no section is left partial by this branch, because the branch narrows an implementation gap rather than adding a requirement.
Files changed: `Sources/MacAgentCore/` — new: `ShellSurfaceDetector.swift` (`ShellSurfaceSignal`, `ShellSurfaceVerdict`, the detector); modified: `LocalRedactionService.swift` (`RedactedPayload.shellSurface`, produced in both producers), `VisionSessionContainment.swift` (the `screenShowsShell` refusal, its sentence, its reason code), `VisionSessionRunner.swift` (the refusal at the redaction step). `Tests/MacAgentCoreTests/` — new: `ShellSurfaceDetectorTests.swift` (the corpus, the threshold, the structural pins); modified: `LocalRedactionServiceTests.swift` (`CaptureShellVerdictTests`), `VisionSessionContainmentTests.swift` (the new refusal, plus the compile-time enforcement described below). `Tests/MacAgentTests/VisionSessionRunTests.swift` (the recognizer seam and the end-to-end wiring tests). No file on the ticket's never-touch list was opened: `git diff --name-only origin/main..HEAD` returns nine files — the eight above plus this changelog — and none of them is `ScreenControlEligibility.swift`, `ScreenControlEligibilityTests.swift`, `RiskApproval.swift`, `SecretTextDetector.swift`, or any Settings surface.
Tests: `env CLANG_MODULE_CACHE_PATH=… swift test --disable-sandbox -Xswiftc -F …` (CLAUDE.md's exact flagged command) -> pass, **1263 tests in 95 suites**, exit 0, at `e8f5fb9`. Branch-point baseline **1224 in 92 suites, exit 0 at `1890ec7`** (`origin/main`), measured on this worktree by this session rather than inherited. Net **+39 tests / +3 suites**; nothing removed. Three pre-existing assertions changed across the three rounds, each explained below because the reason it had to change is itself a finding: `everyRefusalCarriesADistinctReasonCodeAndANonEmptySentence`'s refusal list, `noRecognisedTextLeavesTheServiceWithTheVerdict`'s loop body, and `onlyTheOhMyZshArrowCountsAsAGlyphPrompt`'s expectation once the arrow pattern began consuming oh-my-zsh's own decorations. **Mutation battery: 21 mutations, 21 killed, against the committed tree `11ec2e8`** (pre-rebase; the rebase replayed every patch identically, `git range-diff` reporting `=` on all seven commits). Eight are the original set, six were added at cycle 2 — one per cause the first review found — and seven at cycle 3, one per rule the re-check's findings produced: the sigil glued to a digit again (`aSigilGluedToADigitIsNotAPromptButOneGluedToABracketIs`); any token after the prompt counting as the command again (`onlyTheFirstTokenAfterAPromptCountsAsACommand`); minimal prompts not recognised at all, a single one being enough, the trailing waiting prompt not required, and any occurrence rather than the last one satisfying it (`aMinimalPromptCountsOnlyWhenItRepeatsAndEndsAtAWaitingPrompt`, plus the four `(N2)` corpus fixtures); and the chevron joining the minimal set (`theMinimalPromptSetExcludesTheMarkdownAndBulletGlyphs`, and the bullet-list fixture). One mutation survived its first run at cycle 2 and is recorded in the pitfalls; at cycle 3 every one died on the first pass.

**Cost, measured as a delta at this branch's own SHAs, and the honest answer is still "below the noise floor".** The end-to-end redaction test run *in isolation*, five runs each, both trees measured back to back in one session: `d44900d` (with the check) 391 / 370 / 398 / 408 / 382 ms, mean 390; `1890ec7` (without it) 422 / 386 / 372 / 340 / 366 ms, mean 377. The 13 ms gap is a fifth of either tree's own run-to-run spread, so the instrument cannot resolve the addition — as at cycle 2, where the same measurement put both trees at 314. The check's own cost is therefore measured directly: **3.9–4.3 ms per call** isolated, printed as `SHELL-CHECK-PER-CALL-MICROSECONDS` on every run. The benchmark behind that figure now runs **20 iterations rather than 200**: at 200 it burned ~0.8 s of CPU beside a `VisionSessionRunTests` that gates on fixed three-second wall-clock deadlines, and on this tree eight full-suite runs went from 0 passing to 6. It costs the figure nothing (3,925–4,029 µs at 20 against 3,911–4,261 µs at 200). **This supersedes a claim in the cycle-2 record**, which said cutting the benchmark was "worse on its own terms" because the figure got noisier; that was measured on the lighter cycle-2 tree and does not reproduce here. **It has grown across the three rounds — roughly 3.5 ms at cycle 1, 3.9–4.3 ms now — and the reason is that the prompt signal went from three patterns to six** (the bracketed form, the extended arrow, and the minimal-prompt scan), which is what the two review rounds bought. Recorded rather than optimised: it sits beside an OCR pass costing hundreds of milliseconds, inside a loop that already sleeps 800 ms between iterations. Two instrument notes a future reader needs. The 374 ms figure this branch was told to measure against was recorded at `4e430f0`, before SONNY-114 rewrote the encoder, so the delta is stated against a branch-point number measured here. And the numbers above are *isolated* runs: under the full parallel suite the same figures read far higher (a redaction latency of ~864 ms and a shell-check cost of ~4.8 ms were both observed by the cycle-3 reviewer), because Swift Testing runs suites concurrently. That is a property of the instrument, not of the code, and it is the same caveat as at cycle 2.

Behavior added:
- **Sonny ends a screen-control session when the window it captured is showing a shell.** In all three modes, with the same sentence reaching the panel and the record, and the reason code `screen_shows_shell`. Nothing is sent to the vision provider and no input is synthesized: the refusal sits at the redaction step, above the Safe-mode capture review, the prompt, the send and `perform`.
- **It runs on every capture, not once per session**, at the same cadence as the deny-list re-check — so a shell that appears in the window mid-run ends the session on the next iteration.
- **`ShellSurfaceDetector` reads the joined OCR document already produced for redaction** and reports six independent classes of evidence: a shell prompt, a command run at one (or through a notebook `!` escape), `ls`-style output, a shell's own diagnostic, session chrome, and an interpreter being named as what will run something. **Two distinct classes** are required, as `signalThreshold`.
- **A twenty-six-fixture corpus**, meant to be appended to like `VisionPromptInjectionTests`: twelve that must refuse and fourteen that must not, with the **exact signal set pinned on both halves**. The must-refuse half covers the five the ticket names, an unlisted terminal running oh-my-zsh, an ssh session inside one, a bracketed Linux prompt, and four screens where *nothing has failed* — an idle VS Code panel after `ls`, one with a dev server running, a clean JetBrains run, and a notebook whose commands all worked. The must-not-refuse half covers the three the ticket names plus a README, an email thread with `>` quoted replies, a Dockerfile, Sonny's own Security & Access page, and six pages carrying UI glyphs or address-shaped tokens in prose.

Behavior preserved (required, no blanket claims):
- **The static deny list still refuses first and is still ten entries.** `ScreenControlPolicy.terminalBundleIdentifiers` is untouched, its three doors are untouched, and `checkIterationStart`'s per-iteration re-read is untouched. `aListedTerminalIsStillRefusedByNameBeforeAnyCaptureIsTaken` pins the ordering as behaviour: a Terminal target is refused with the *terminal* sentence and the scripted recognizer is never called even once, because the adapter answers before a capture is taken.
- **`aTerminalNobodyListedIsControllableAndThatIsTheKnownGap` is intact and unweakened.** It still asserts the gap, and this branch does not let anyone read a green suite as proof of the categorical rule.
- **`RedactedPayload`'s structural non-bypass.** The initializer is still `fileprivate`, the type is still not `Codable`, every field is still `let`, and `onlyTheServiceProducesPayloadsInTheLiveModule` still names exactly one production file. The new field rides inside that boundary rather than around it.
- **Fail-closed redaction, unchanged and now load-bearing for a second thing.** A recognizer that throws, a region outside the image, and an undecodable capture all still produce an error and no payload; `recognizerFailureFailsClosedWithNoPayload` is untouched and still passes.
- **Every existing vision-session flow.** All 51 tests in `VisionSessionRunTests` pass, including the ones this branch did not add: the ordinary Normal-mode run, Safe mode's envelope → capture review → action approval sequence, delegation in all four of its shapes, the emergency stop, the pause/resume machinery, the iteration cap, and the SONNY-114 resampled-coordinate pins.
- **The consequence rule, `RiskApprovalPolicy.requirement(for:context:)` and `ApprovalContext`** are untouched — this branch adds a refusal, not an approval axis.
- **`SecretTextDetector`'s classes and patterns** are untouched. The shell detector is a sibling file, not an extension of them.

Architectural decisions / pitfalls discovered (required):
- **Two independent signs means two independent *classes*, and the first design failed that on its own terms.** A `promptScrollback` signal — fires when three or more lines match the prompt shape — was written, measured against the whole corpus, and then deleted. It worked: every must-refuse fixture passed with a sign to spare. It was wrong anyway, because counting prompt lines once as "a prompt is present" and again as "several prompts are present" is one class of evidence producing a refusal by itself, which is exactly what the founder's condition forbids. Removing it cost the corpus nothing. `repeatingOneClassOfEvidenceNeverReachesTheThresholdByItself` now pins the property so the next signal cannot quietly reintroduce it.
- **No signal subtracts, and that is a security property rather than a simplification.** `.claude/rules/macagentcore-conventions.md` already says screen-derived signals may add scrutiny and never remove it. A "this looks like source code, never mind" suppressor would be a string an attacker paints on the screen to switch the check off. The one pattern that declines to fire on text it otherwise matches — the shebang exclusion in `shellInterpreterInvocation` — is a statement about what that text *is* (a file's first line, not a command being run) and cancels nothing found elsewhere. The VS Code fixture carries a shebang in its editor half deliberately, and still refuses on the panel below it.
- **The sharpest false positive is a `.sh` file open in an editor, and the fixture is built to be adversarial rather than convenient.** Line 11 of it is `scp "$f" "deploy@$1:$APP_DIR/"` — the closest thing in ordinary shell source to a `user@host:path$` prompt. It lands at **zero** signals, not one, and `aShellScriptOpenInAnEditorIsNotAnInteractiveShell` asserts emptiness rather than merely "does not refuse", so a fixture drifting to one sign fails loudly instead of passing with no margin.
- **A vocabulary is not specificity, and believing it was is the mistake this branch actually made.** The first design read `shellCommandNames` after *any* line-start sigil, and its own comment argued that requiring a real command name is what let quoted email through. The reviewer measured it: of sixteen quoted-reply lines whose first word is in the list, **fifteen fired the signal** — `go`, `make`, `open`, `find`, `top`, `clear`, `exit`, `history`, `man`, `kill`, `head`, `source`, `java`, `touch`, `less`. The only one that did not was `> can you run …`, which is the exact phrasing the committed email fixture used. The vocabulary was doing nothing, because ordinary English is full of command names; what does the work is **position**. The list is now consulted only in the remainder of a real prompt line, or after a notebook `!`. The words stay in it deliberately — after `%` on a prompt they are commands, and removing them would blind the check to `% make release` and `% go test ./...` — and they are now harmless, because a blockquote has no prompt. **The general lesson, since this repo has met it before in another coat: a filter that is specific about its *vocabulary* and vague about its *context* is not specific.**
- **The verdict is non-optional and there is deliberately no "could not tell" state.** An `Optional` or a third enum case would be permission granted by omission — a `nil` a reader treats as "no shell" is precisely the answer an unreadable screen must not produce. Both of `LocalRedactionService`'s producers fill the field from the one detector over the text they actually had, so there is no capture-only special case with an inert value on the other path, and no branch where the field is defaulted.
- **Fail-closed is inherited structurally rather than re-implemented, and the test asserts the session *ends*.** `redactCapture` already threw `detectionUnavailable` when recognition failed, so an unreadable screen already ended the session; the shell verdict simply cannot exist without a successful scan. `anUnreadableScreenEndsTheSessionRatherThanProducingANoShellVerdict` asserts the session outcome, never that a verdict came back clean — the mutation that maps a recognizer failure to an empty observation list is killed by it.
- **Swift `Regex` cost is compilation, not matching, and the two obvious fixes were both rejected.** Measured here: evaluating a regex literal costs roughly 200 µs of compilation against roughly 90 µs of matching, and the pattern's shape barely moves either number — a lazy-bounded prompt pattern and a negated-class rewrite of it came out at 288 µs and 283 µs. Caching the compiled patterns in `nonisolated(unsafe) static let`s was rejected because `Regex` is not `Sendable` for a real reason (it lazily builds a lowered program inside itself) and the suite runs tests in parallel; a documented data race in the file that decides whether Sonny may drive a window is not worth 3 ms. Cheap substring prefilters in front of the expensive patterns were rejected because a prefilter is a second, weaker copy of a pattern's necessary condition, and when the two drift the signal stops firing *silently* — a fail-open bug in a check whose entire job is to fail closed. What was taken is the version with no drift surface at all: signals that are fixed strings use `String.contains`, which is about twelve times cheaper than the simplest regex and is the whole check rather than a filter in front of one. That alone took the per-call cost from 4.07 ms to 3.4 ms.
- **A test that claimed to be exhaustive was not, and this branch is what proved it.** `everyRefusalCarriesADistinctReasonCodeAndANonEmptySentence`'s doc comment promised that "a new ending has to come here and choose a code rather than silently borrowing one". `screenShowsShell` was added to `VisionContainmentRefusal` and the suite stayed green: the list is hand-written, so "exhaustively" described how it had been typed and not anything enforced. Fixed rather than merely appended to — `expectedReasonCode(for:)` is an exhaustive `switch` over the enum, so a new case now fails to compile in the file whose job is to make sure it picked a code. Same family as `identifierProducing(_:)` in `ScreenControlEligibilityTests`. **That fix was itself incomplete and the reviewer caught it** — see the bullet on compiler-forced switches below.
- **The signals were the defect and the threshold was not, and telling those apart is the whole of the cycle-2 fix** (PR #57 F1/F2, coordinator adjudication 2026-08-17). The review found the check refusing documentation pages, GitHub issues, Notion runbooks, slide decks, mail threads and Slack, *and* allowing a VS Code terminal panel whenever the last command happened to succeed. Those read as opposite pressures on `signalThreshold` and are not: they are one defect, in signals that were not specific to what they claimed to detect. The prompt signal accepted `▶` and `»` — a disclosure triangle and a breadcrumb separator — and fired on any line holding an address-shaped token followed anywhere later by `$`, `%` or `#`, so "alice@example.com says the build is 50% faster now" was a prompt. The command signal fired on a bare sigil at line start. Making each specific resolved both at once, with the founder's two-signs rule untouched: a real prompt plus a command typed at it is two classes for an idle panel after `ls`, and a documentation page carrying a lone `$` is now **zero**. All eight of the reviewer's repros land at zero and none of the sixteen quoted replies fires. **Lowering the threshold would have traded one false negative for a worse false positive** — it is precisely what keeps a single `$` from stopping real work — which is why it is now documented on the constant itself as not being the tuning surface.
- **`%%bash` is an interpreter selection, not a command echo, and separating them is what makes a clean notebook refuse.** Both facts were originally read by the same signal, so a notebook where every command succeeded reached one class. Naming an interpreter and running a command are different facts about the world, and a notebook that does both should not have them counted as one — the same reasoning that deleted `promptScrollback`, applied in the other direction.
- **A test the corpus could not fail is not coverage, and the corpus is now built so that it can.** The seven negative fixtures contained no UI glyph, no address-shaped token in prose beside a `%`, and no quoted line whose first word was in the vocabulary; the seven positive ones contained no shell in which nothing had gone wrong. A green corpus was therefore compatible with both defects. It is now twenty-six fixtures, the **exact signal set is asserted on both halves** rather than only the verdict, six of the additions are the reviewer's own repros verbatim, and `theCorpusCoversBothDirections` asserts by name that the F1 and F2 fixtures stay present so a later trim cannot quietly remove what made the corpus able to fail.
- **A structural guarantee needs a structural test, and "I enumerated it" is not one.** The claim that only six fixed strings ever cross the verdict boundary was true and checked by hand — and nothing in the suite would have failed if someone had later added `public let matchedLine: String` to help debug a false positive. That is the leak shape this repository has shipped twice. It is now pinned two ways: `theVerdictHoldsNothingButSignalsAndThatIsCheckedNotAssumed` strips comments from the declaration and asserts the **complete** property list, so any addition fails rather than only the ones a keyword list anticipated; and `twoDifferentScreensWithTheSameSignalsProduceEqualVerdicts` asserts it behaviourally, since a field carrying screen text would make two screens sharing no words compare unequal. The loop it replaced asserted that screen text does not contain the string `interactive_prompt`, which is true whatever the verdict carries.
- **A compiler-forced `switch` in front of a hand-written array forces the author into the file and not into the array.** Round one of the exhaustive-refusal fix added `expectedReasonCode(for:)`, an exhaustive `switch`, and the reviewer pointed out the remaining hole: a new case could take a `switch` arm and never a sample, and then its distinct-code and non-empty-sentence assertions simply never ran while a literal `== 12` kept passing. Closed with a `CaseIterable` tag enum and mappings in **both** directions — the real enum maps to a tag, the tag maps to a sample, the array is built from `allCases`, and the count literal is gone. Neither direction can be satisfied without the other.
- **The battery's one survivor was the round's most useful result.** Fourteen mutations, thirteen killed on the first run; the fourteenth — dropping `(?m)` from the colon-form prompt pattern — changed nothing anywhere in the suite. The reason is subtle and worth carrying: without `(?m)` the `$` in the trailing lookahead means end of *input*, and every colon-form prompt in the corpus happened to be either followed by a space or the document's last line. The uncovered case is the ordinary one — a shell that has printed its prompt and is waiting, with scrollback below it. Fixed with a test rather than a note, and the battery then killed all fourteen.
- **Narrowing a signal to kill false positives can silently take out the feature's own subject, and only a measurement finds that** (PR #57 N2). Cycle 2 made the prompt signal specific, which fixed the false refusals — and removed *any* prompt lacking `user@host`, `bash-N.N`, `➜` or `PS C:\`. Four shapes went from refusing to proceeding and nothing on the branch recorded it; the limitations entry named one glyph ("starship") where the effect was a prompt-style class. That is the more dangerous half: the deny list already covers terminal *applications*, so this check's entire subject is shells inside something else, and those render whatever prompt the user's shell renders — a bare `$ ` being among the commonest. **The lesson is about the record as much as the code**: a limitation written as the theme that prompted it, rather than as the population that was measured, reads as much smaller than it is.
- **The fix for that had to be tightened twice, by documents built to break it.** A minimal `$ `/`% ` prompt is recognised when it repeats and the **last** occurrence is bare. Repetition alone refuses a documentation page with two `$` examples; "some occurrence is bare" alone refuses a LaTeX or config comment block, which uses a bare `%` as a separator — and one of those reached the command signal on the word `source`. Requiring the *last* occurrence to be the bare one is what distinguishes a scrollback, which ends where the cursor sits, from a comment block, which does not. Both halves have a fixture and a mutation.
- **`❯` is a bullet before it is a prompt, and that is a measured trade rather than a preference.** Admitting the chevron to the minimal set under the identical rule refuses an ordinary agenda list ending in an empty bullet — the F1 class this branch already paid for once. So starship, pure and powerlevel10k stay unrecognised, the bullet-list document is kept as a corpus fixture so the trade stays visible, and the bound is stated with the measurement instead of being inherited. `#` is excluded for the neighbouring reason: it is Markdown's heading marker, and a root prompt arrives as `root@host:/#` through the colon form anyway.
- **Two signals can share a failure mode without sharing a class, and that is not independence** (PR #57 N1). `commandRunInAShell`'s prompt half is computed *from* the prompt ranges, so a prompt misfire produced both signals off one line with no second opinion — `priya@acme.io 82% open`, which is what a support-desk or CRM table OCRs to, refused on its own. The derivation is inherent to the case that fixed F2 (a command typed at a prompt is on the prompt's own line), so "require different lines" was not available. What was available was shrinking the shared surface: the sigil may no longer be glued to the character before it unless that character closes a bracket, and the command must be the **first** token after the prompt rather than any of ninety names anywhere in the remainder. **The residual is recorded as an assertion, not as prose** — the invoice line still reads as a prompt shape and now yields one signal instead of two, so the fix removes the refusal and not the misread.
- **A rebase measurement is invalid if the tree was dirty, and the script will not say so.** A paired latency comparison printed the same SHA for both columns because `git checkout --detach origin/main` had failed against an uncommitted changelog edit, so the "branch point" column was actually the branch. Caught by reading the SHA the script printed rather than only the numbers under it. Stamp the SHA on every column, and read it.
- **Safe mode's session-envelope approval comes before the first capture, and that does not contradict the design's "the first capture happens before the control approval".** They are different gates: the envelope approval is the *plan*-level tier-3 gate that already ships, while the sentence in `docs/sonny-row-j-plan.md` §4.3 is about the per-app control consent row J's later branches add. The two are easy to read as contradicting each other. `aWindowShowingAShellEndsTheSessionInEveryMode` answers the envelope approval explicitly in its Safe arm and says why in place.
- **Three of the ticket's own `Sources/` citations had moved before this branch started, and were re-read rather than trusted.** SONNY-114 merged hours earlier: `redactCapture` is `:161-239` not `:146-220`, its fail-closed throw is `:169-172` not `:154-156`, and `RedactedPayload.redactedImagePNGData` is now `redactedImageData` plus a media type and dimensions. Nothing about the design changed, which is the point — the citations were checked because they *could* have changed, not because something looked wrong.

Known limitations / deferred scope:
- **This does not close SONNY-102 and no record of this work may say it does.** A screen check reads exactly the surface an attacker controls, which is why the deny list stays load-bearing and is not trimmed by one entry; and a shell that is not rendered is not seen. SONNY-102 stays open at high priority.
- **What the check does not reach**, stated as what was measured rather than as a theme:
  - **Structural, and permanent:** a shell in a background tab, a collapsed panel, a scrolled-away region, or any window other than the captured one.
  - **A prompt with nothing typed at it** is one sign and proceeds. So is **a prompt carrying a command `shellCommandNames` does not know** — `% mytool --deploy` is one sign, while `% ./mytool --deploy` is two, because `./` is a shell construct rather than a name that has to be listed. Pinned by `aPromptAloneAndAnUnrecognisedCommandBothStayAtOneSign`.
  - **Prompt styles the prompt signal does not know.** The signal recognises `user@host` (colon and spaced forms), `bash-N.N`, oh-my-zsh's `➜`, PowerShell's `PS C:\…>`, and — since PR #57's N2 — a repeated minimal `$ `/`% ` ending at a waiting prompt. **Anything else is invisible.** The concrete casualty is any prompt whose only glyph is `❯`, which is starship, pure and powerlevel10k in their default configurations — the three most common custom prompts on a developer's Mac. **Said as what it costs a person rather than as a list of prompt themes: a developer running starship inside a VS Code panel is not caught by the screen check.** That is the exact case this feature exists for — the static deny list already refuses terminal *applications*, so an embedded shell is the whole remaining subject — and it is a gap Sonny walks straight past. Measured, not assumed: `❯ ./scripts/deploy.sh` / `zsh: permission denied: …` / `❯` yields `shell_diagnostic` alone and proceeds, asserted by `theMinimalPromptSetExcludesTheMarkdownAndBulletGlyphs`.
  - **Excluding `❯` is a founder decision of 2026-08-17, taken on a measurement, and the cost was named before it was accepted.** It is a bullet glyph before it is a prompt glyph. Admitting it to the minimal set — under the same repetition-plus-waiting-prompt rule that made `$ ` and `% ` safe — was tried and refuses an ordinary agenda list ending in an empty bullet, which is the F1 class this branch already paid for once. **The founder's reasoning, recorded on SONNY-139 so it is not re-litigated: a false stop is worse than a gap**, because a stop is unappealable and the product is not allowed to explain why it happened, while the gap is partly covered by the deny list catching terminal applications outright. Two alternatives were declined: adding the glyph, and spending another round hunting a discriminator that separates a prompt-with-scrollback from a bullet list. The bullet-list document is kept as a corpus fixture (`a bullet list ending in an empty chevron`) so the trade stays visible rather than becoming folklore. `#` is excluded for a neighbouring reason: it is Markdown's heading marker and most config formats' comment marker, and a root prompt arrives through the colon form as `root@host:/#` anyway.
  - **(Corrected 2026-08-17, PR #57 N2.)** This entry previously read only "a starship prompt is also not recognised", which named one theme when the measured effect was a prompt-style *class*: the cycle-3 re-check found four shapes that had gone from refusing to proceeding when the prompt signal was narrowed at cycle 2 — a minimal `$ ` prompt with a failed command, a starship prompt with a failed command, a minimal `$ ` prompt with `ls -l` output, and a bare `% ` prompt with `ls -l` output. **Three of those four are now fixed** by the repeated-minimal-prompt rule and are corpus fixtures marked `(N2)`; the fourth is the `❯` case above, which is the residual. Recording one glyph where the effect was a class is the error being corrected, and it mattered because the deny list already covers terminal *applications* — this check exists for shells inside something else, which show whatever prompt the user's shell shows.
  - **(Corrected 2026-08-17, PR #57 F2.)** This bullet first read "…a window showing a prompt and nothing else, which is one sign and proceeds … bounded in practice by the check running on every capture — the moment anything runs in that shell, a second class of evidence appears and the session ends." **The final clause was false when written**, and the reviewer measured it: `ls`, `cd`, `cd Desktop`, `git status`, `swift build` and `python3 train.py` each produced a full shell session that stayed at one sign and proceeded, because the only things that added a second class were an `ls -l`-shaped listing or something failing. The mitigation the bound leaned on did not exist. It was load-bearing for the disposition "a bare prompt proceeding is acceptable", which is why it is corrected here rather than quietly dropped. All six now refuse — a command typed at a real prompt is itself the second class — and `aSucceedingCommandAtAPromptIsTwoSigns` asserts each of the five the reviewer named. The bound that replaces it is the one stated above, and it is narrower: not "anything runs", but "a *recognised* command, or any `./` execution, runs".
- **The window-scoping guarantee is load-bearing for "end the session" and must be revisited if it ever changes.** `SCContentFilter(desktopIndependentWindow:)` is still the only content filter in the repository, so a terminal behind Chrome cannot end a Chrome session. Any change that widens the capture — multi-window, per-display, a region grab — invalidates the refusal's disposition rather than merely affecting it.
- **An Accessibility-tree signal is deliberately not added.** Recorded during planning as later hardening, not as an omission: it would add a second permission that can be revoked mid-session and a second failure mode, for a second view of a question the OCR pass already answers locally and fail-closed.
- **No new product copy**, per the founder's 2026-08-14 rule. Settings › Security & Access still says only "Sonny will never control Terminal, iTerm, or any other terminal app"; nothing in the app explains that the screen is also read.

Open questions (required): none. Everything the design left open was settled on SONNY-104's answers comment and implemented as recorded. What remains is the founder's five manual items on SONNY-139 — above all whether a real VS Code window with a visible terminal panel refuses while the same window showing a `.sh` file proceeds, which no test on this branch can answer because no test drives the real app.

Next branch: `feature/app-control-consent` (SONNY-140/141/142/143), then `feature/app-control-revocation` (SONNY-144). Row J's three branches are sequential by the founder's own analysis — all three touch the same Settings page and the same vision adapter, so a disjointness check does not pass.

### Branch: fix/sonny-114-capture-payload
Status: complete
Date: 2026-08-17
Tickets: SONNY-114 (vision captures were unbounded full-resolution PNG — measured, re-encoded for egress, and the coordinate space the model answers in separated from the capture's)
Reviewed by: fresh session (per WORKFLOW.md step 7) — pending at PR open

Spec sections covered: none new. This is a correctness and cost fix inside §13's screen-control loop and §12.3's redaction path; the behaviour the spec describes is unchanged.
Files changed: `Sources/MacAgentCore/` — new: `RedactedCaptureEncoder`; modified: `LocalRedactionService` (the payload's image fields, and the paint/encode call — `RedactionImageRenderer` moved into the new file and is gone from this one), `VisionModelClient`, `ScreenActionSynthesizer` (`SentImageSize` + `VisionPointResolver`), `VisionSessionRunner`, `VisionSessionEnvironment`, `VisionSessionJournalStore` (doc only). `Sources/MacAgent/` — `FloatingWidgetView` (one field rename). `Tests/` — new: `RedactedCaptureEncoderTests`, `VisionModelClientTests`, `ImageFixtures` (extracted from `LocalRedactionServiceTests` so two suites share one sampler); modified: `LocalRedactionServiceTests`, `VisionDecisionAndGeometryTests`, `VisionSessionRunTests`. `.claude/rules/macagentcore-conventions.md`.
Tests: `env CLANG_MODULE_CACHE_PATH=… swift test --disable-sandbox -Xswiftc -F …` (CLAUDE.md's exact flagged command) -> pass, **1222 tests in 92 suites**, exit 0, at `b07bee8`. Branch-point baseline at `2c51f4d` was 1205 in 90 suites. (The branch was cut from `9a84e3b`, where the baseline was 1203 in 90 suites, and rebased onto `2c51f4d` after rows 12 and J's planning and `fix/sonny-103-containment-test-injection` merged; SONNY-103's two added tests are the difference. The rebase replayed both commits unchanged: `git range-diff e260575~1..e260575 b07bee8~1..b07bee8` reports `1: e260575 = 1: b07bee8`, the `=` meaning an identical patch. **Corrected 2026-08-17, coordinator finding.** This first cited `git diff e260575 b07bee8` as being empty. It is not, and it cannot be: `git diff` between two commits compares the *trees* they produce, and a rebase onto a newer `main` necessarily pulls that main's delta into the new tree — run here it reports 6 files and 1,582 insertions, which is SONNY-103's two test files plus rows 12 and J's plan documents arriving. The conclusion was right and the evidence cited could not have produced it, which is the same shape as this repo's count-the-population rule: correct the method, not just the outcome. It also means `Tests/` is *not* identical across the two commits, since SONNY-103's changes to `VisionSessionContainmentTests.swift` and `UnattendedVisionNeverTests.swift` are in `b07bee8`'s tree and not in `e260575`'s. The re-stamped figures are unaffected: they were produced by an external scratchpad package linking `MacAgentCore`, and SONNY-103's delta is confined to two test files in a target that package never builds.)

Behavior added:
- A capture is encoded for egress as both PNG and JPEG q80, and the smaller one is sent. Measured over the five real captures available on the development machine at `9a84e3b`, that is 3,549,073 bytes today against 1,226,249 after — 0.346x — with the largest single capture falling from 1,552,704 to 124,953.
- A capture that will not fit a 3,000,000-byte budget is resampled down a ladder that spends quality before resolution and stops at half scale. The ladder returns the **first** rung that fits, so most captures never leave the top one; nothing real reaches the rest, and of fifteen fixtures only uniform noise at 3440x1440 and 3008x1692 point resolution resampled at all. The reproducible worst case is the seeded noise fixture at a 5K display's point resolution: **2,781,667 bytes at its full 2560x1440, no resampling**, printed by `theShippingPolicyKeepsEvenItsWorstCaseUnderTheCeiling` on every run (measured at `6201e45`). Figures for point resolutions above 5K are deliberately not quoted anywhere — the ones measured for this branch came from a one-off random image nobody can reproduce.
- The request the vision route receives is bounded by construction. The largest JSON body across every fixture measured is 3,512,879 bytes; the arithmetic ceiling for a payload sitting exactly on the cap is 4,000,000 bytes of base64 plus a 4,673-character prompt.
- The coordinate space the model answers in is a type — `SentImageSize` — and the prompt, the bounds check, the point-to-screen scale and the journal all read it.

Behavior preserved (required, no blanket claims):
- **The structural non-bypass.** `RedactedPayload`'s initializer is still `fileprivate` to `LocalRedactionService.swift` and the type is still not `Codable`; the new encoder file constructs no payload, so `onlyTheServiceProducesPayloadsInTheLiveModule` still names exactly one production file.
- **Fail-closed redaction.** A recognizer that throws, a region entirely outside the image, and an undecodable capture all still produce an error and no payload — the same three tests, unchanged, now running through the new encoder.
- **Every region a match touches is still painted whole**, including a private-key block spanning observations (PR #49 F1), and the report still cannot claim a redaction that did not happen (F8).
- **The clear refusal above the ceiling.** `payloadTooLarge` still fails the whole iteration rather than truncating, with the same message shape; only the number it carries changed. It now has its first test.
- **Safe mode's pre-send preview** shows the bytes that will actually leave, at the dimensions they will leave at, which is what it always claimed and is now literally true after a resample.
- **The window title, history, closing rationale and delegated instruction** still go through `redactText` on exactly the paths PR #50's cycles closed; nothing in this branch touched them.
- **Click resolution for an unresampled capture is bit-for-bit what it was.** Every pre-existing geometry expectation (the moved window, the resize tolerance on both sides, own-window suppression, the 2x Retina case, the zero-size guard) asserts the same values through the new signature.

Architectural decisions / pitfalls discovered (required):
- **The capture was already at its minimum sensible resolution, and that inverts the obvious fix.** `ScreenCaptureService` captures at `.nominal` — one pixel per point — so the 2x Retina downscale has already happened and a 13-point label is 13 pixels tall. Measured with the Vision framework's own recognizer: a *lossless* half-scale encode reproduced 3% of a real capture's lines verbatim where a full-resolution JPEG reproduced 85%. "Downscale for the vision model" is the intuitive answer and it is the expensive one. Resolution is the axis to protect; quality is the axis to spend.
- **Lossy is not always smaller, and where it is not is exactly where it hurts.** A full screen of 13-point type encoded 1,174,367 bytes as PNG and 1,432,189 as JPEG q80; a terminal window, 60,390 against 78,315. Dense sharp text is simultaneously PNG's best case and JPEG's worst, and it is also the content class small-text artifacting damages most. So "encode both, send the smaller" gets the lossless-where-it-matters behaviour for free, and no rule has to encode the correlation.
- **A resample changes the coordinate space a model answers in, and three of the four consumers already read the payload while the fourth read the capture.** The prompt and the Safe-mode preview took `payload.imagePixelWidth ?? capture.pixelWidth`; `VisionPointResolver` took `capture.pixelWidth` outright. Under any resample that resolver would have scaled by the wrong factor and every click would have landed short in proportion — inside the window, plausible-looking, wrong. Two `Int` parameters would have let the mistake be made again at the next call site, so the resolver takes a `SentImageSize`.
- **What a resample costs a click is bounded and small; what it costs a *model* is not something a test can answer.** The resolved point is within one sent pixel of the model's target at every rung — at most one point unresampled, two at the 0.5 floor, against a 20-point minimum control. Whether a model aims as well at a smaller picture is the founder's attended run, and the ticket says so rather than implying the arithmetic settled it.
- **The ticket's stated worry about resample-before-redaction came out the other way, and it is recorded rather than dropped.** A misordering that *also rescales the region* leaks nothing at any ladder scale — `LocalRedactionService`'s existing 2-pixel pad is wider than the resample kernel's spread even after shrinking. The pad is doing work nobody had counted on it for and would stop if it were narrowed. What leaks badly (219–231 red dominance on the tracer) is the misordering that forgets to rescale the region, which is the realistic bug, and that is what the ordering test controls against.
- **The flatten to an opaque canvas is required by the format choice, not a tidy-up.** JPEG cannot carry alpha, so without it the two candidate encodings describe different pictures and their byte counts are not comparable. It costs nothing visible: a window capture's only sub-opaque pixels are its rounded corners (356 of them, in both real window captures at `9a84e3b`), they are transparent *black*, and compositing them onto black changed zero pixels' RGB across every fixture measured.
- **A test fixture that lied about its own size was harmless only because nothing decoded it.** `VisionSessionRunTests`' capture backend returned a 1x1 PNG while declaring 800x600. The old redaction path re-encoded only when a region was detected and no test detected one, so the bytes were never opened. The egress encoder decodes every capture, so the payload's dimensions became the image's own and 27 tests went red at once — correctly. Fixtures whose declared metadata is not derived from their content are a latent break waiting for the first consumer that looks.
- **ImageIO's JPEG encoder subsamples chroma 4:2:0 at every quality up to 0.95**, switching to 4:4:4 only at 1.0 — measured off the SOF marker at each quality. It is not a problem here (UI text carries its contrast in luminance, which subsampling leaves alone, and q100 is usually larger than the PNG candidate so the smaller-wins rule picks lossless anyway), but a future reader tuning quality should know the knob is not continuous.
- **`CGImageDestination` on macOS 26.5 will not create a WebP destination at all**, so the format is not available to choose regardless of what the route accepts. HEIC encodes and is by far the smallest (0.18x of PNG on a real capture) and was still not adopted: the route's acceptance of it is unverified, and a format the far end rejects is a failed iteration, not a saving.

Known limitations / deferred scope:
- The per-iteration cost of the encode step is 14.5 ms at 0.26 megapixels to 250 ms at 5.1 megapixels, 80–130 ms on the real 2-megapixel captures, measured at `b07bee8`. It is paid on every capture now rather than only on captures containing a secret, and it buys back far more than it costs on any real uplink.
- Request compression is not done here and is filed instead: deflating the finished JSON body recovers 29–35% losslessly on the post-change payloads (measured at `b07bee8` on the five real captures), almost all of it the base64 overhead.
- The half-pixel sampling convention in `VisionPointResolver` is pre-existing, measured here, and filed rather than changed — it would alter the acting loop beyond this ticket's scope.

Open questions (required): none. The encoding and the budget are decided on the evidence above; what remains is the founder's attended check that a real model aims as well at a JPEG as at a PNG, which is a manual-test item on the ticket rather than an open decision.

Next branch: row 12's host choice. Its planning landed at `2c51f4d` while this branch was in flight and explicitly holds the choice itself for this number — `docs/sonny-row-12-plan.md` §3.3 names the ~12,005,000-byte body as "an accident, not a design" and gates SONNY-125 on this ticket. The replacement figure is about 4.01 MB.
