Behavior added: none. This branch ships decisions and ticket contracts, nothing executable.

Architectural decisions / pitfalls discovered (required, write "none" if true):

**The substance lives in two documents, not here, deliberately.** `docs/sonny-founder-design-decisions.md`'s new "Workspace restriction scope (roadmap row B)" section is the durable decision record and the thing to read first; `docs/sonny-branch-b-plan.md` is the full rationale behind the seven ticket contracts, including the per-operation resource-classification table. Restating either here would create a third copy that drifts. What follows is only what a reader of this changelog needs in order to know whether to go read them.

**The scope verdict is four-valued, and that is the whole resolution of SONNY-12's recorded interaction flag.** `.inScope` / `.outOfScope` / `.unconstrained` / `.opaque`. A resource kind a workspace never configured is `.unconstrained` — it produces no new prompts and is *never eligible for branch C's relaxation*. So "users who never configured scope get relaxed tier-3 gating implicitly" becomes structurally impossible rather than something a toggle has to prevent, and no separate per-workspace opt-in is needed. Both two-valued readings fail against state that already exists on disk: "empty means deny all" would escalate every file action in every workspace on day one (no workspace has file locations — the field does not exist yet), and "empty means allow all" would blanket-bless every path, which is exactly the hazard the flag named. `CreateWorkspaceCapabilityAdapter` requires *at least one* of apps or URLs (`guard !apps.isEmpty || !urls.isEmpty` throws only when both are empty), so apps-only and URLs-only workspaces are already normal valid states that a two-valued model would break on the kind they never declared.

**Two hard constraints handed to branch C, recorded in the decisions doc and in row C's roadmap cell.** First, relaxation must be a *requirement* override and must never lower `effectiveTier` — the unattended gate (`approvedTier >= effectiveTier` against a fixed `.approved(.tier2)`), the stale-approval re-check, the `risk.assessed`/`risk.escalated` trace, and `UnattendedTrustAdvisory` all read that field, and lowering it would let in-scope tier-3 work run on a schedule with nobody present. Second, the in-scope tier-2 and tier-3 rules must be one function of `(tier, verdict)`, never two chained rules: branch C decided both that in-scope tier 2 auto-runs and that in-scope tier 3 drops to a lightweight confirmation, so implementing the tier-3 rule as a tier change (3 -> 2) would let the tier-2 rule fire next and make in-scope tier-3 actions silently auto-run. That is the single most likely way this feature ships as a security regression.

**The founder's escalate-and-prompt decision needs no new machinery, which is the largest thing this planning phase de-risked.** `AgentRunner.execute` gates once, entirely up front — the requirement is checked before `executor.execute(plan:)` is ever invoked — and `executeChain` runs every segment with no re-gating, which `.claude/rules/macagentcore-conventions.md` forbids adding. So "this isn't part of X workspace — allow anyway?" lands as a pre-execution, whole-plan prompt identical in shape and timing to every existing risk escalation. No pause/resume/checkpoint infrastructure. The prompt copy is a `CapabilityRiskEscalation.reason`, which both approval surfaces already render as a standalone line — a new escalation *source*, not a new mechanism.

**`.opaque` is the honest name for what a pre-execution gate cannot see, and it is load-bearing rather than a fudge.** A Shortcut's internals (Sonny shells to `shortcuts run <name>`) and a search-driven `web_to_markdown`'s real URLs (returned by the search provider mid-execution) are unknowable when the one gate fires. They never escalate on scope grounds and are never eligible for relaxation, and a plan-level roll-up is `.inScope` only if no step is `.opaque` — so a plan containing a Shortcut can never be relaxed by association with resources that did match. Treating an unseeable resource as in-scope would have the feature grant exactly the permissions it cannot verify.

**Resource classification must be per operation *including implicit resources*, never per field.** Three app/URL touches bypass every existing allowlist with no plan field naming them: `MicrosoftWordDocumentConverter` AppleScript-controls Word from a hardcoded path (`DocumentConverter.swift:71-100`), `NativeMediaOpener` opens `music://`/`spotify:` URIs through its own private prefix check bypassing both `MacAppCatalog` and `SafeURL` (`MediaPlaybackService.swift:985-1035`), and `AppleScriptFinderContextReader` controls Finder. `open_app_search_url` is the mirror-image trap: it carries `appName`, but that field holds a search-target name like "GitHub" and its real resource is the host baked into the template. A field-driven classifier would report all four plans as touching nothing.

**`WorkspaceStore.save` fully replaces the record, unlike `RoutineStore.save`.** Re-creating a workspace by natural language would silently delete its scope configuration — and that path escalates to tier 3 *for replacing the workspace*, so the user consents to replacing contents, not to dropping a security boundary. SONNY-36 carries the merge-preserve fix. This is the same class of bug `RoutineStore.save` already solved for `schedule`/`recentRunDates`, and it was found by comparing the two stores rather than by reading either alone.

**One real spec conflict, resolved rather than papered over.** §10.4 lists "Scope validation" among seven validation layers with "If any layer rejects, the action must not execute" — a hard block that contradicts escalate-and-prompt on a literal reading. Resolution: two different layers, both correct. §10.1/§10.4's scope is the *capability's own* declared operating bounds and stays a hard block; workspace scope is a new *user-declared* layer above it that escalates. A workspace scope can never widen a capability's own bounds or `PathWhitelist` — it only narrows, and it narrows by asking. One consequence worth carrying: a workspace file location outside `~/Desktop`/`~/Documents` is inert, because `PathWhitelist` rejects the path before scope is consulted, so it must be validated and reported at save time.

**A configuration surface is what makes this a feature at all.** Spec §6.9 requires "Edit workspace"; it does not exist, and without it `fileLocations` can never be set, every file resource stays `.unconstrained` forever, and one of the three founder-specified dimensions does nothing. The user chose to ship both halves: SONNY-40 (natural-language editing) and SONNY-41 (a detail sheet that makes the boundary visible). SONNY-41 is net-new UI with no wireframe — `13-MainAppWorkspaces.svg` is a card grid only — built in System A, explicitly *not* inheriting the routine-detail view's System-B-inside-System-A glass treatment, because liquid glass was tried for workspace cards and reverted. Recorded as a stated exception per CLAUDE.md's wireframe rule.

**The persistent active-workspace rejection is not being reversed, and the difference is entirely lifecycle.** "Started from its card" does not exist today — `openWorkspaceWidget` synthesizes the literal `"Open my X workspace"`, so the card starts exactly one kind of task and is not even a distinct signal from the free-text path. SONNY-39 adds a real per-task dispatch: set at dispatch, cleared at terminal state, never inherited by the next command, never a global mode. The wireframes' "Active" badge, Open-vs-Switch branching, and Home's "Personal" scope pill stay unbuilt. SONNY-38 and SONNY-39 are the two tickets a reviewer should check hardest against this.

Known limitations / deferred scope:

- **Nothing implemented.** This is a planning branch; all seven contracts are unstarted. SONNY-36 is unblocked now; SONNY-37 onward is gated on SONNY-29.
- **Hard sequencing dependency on SONNY-29**, recorded on SONNY-37 and in row B's roadmap cell. SONNY-29 rewrites `AgentActionExecutor.assessRisk` into a segment-walking shape and is first in the declared serial SONNY-29 -> SONNY-31 -> SONNY-24 sequence over that surface. This is not only a merge-conflict concern: today `assessRisk` hands each deduplicated adapter the entire unsegmented plan once, so a scope check built on that shape would check the first URL in a two-URL plan and silently ignore the second. SONNY-37 carries an acceptance criterion whose whole job is to prove the rebase happened.
- **`save_routine`'s scoped resources are classified as none** — saving a routine touches one file inside Sonny's own store, and the routine's steps are scoped when it actually runs. This answers, for scope, the same "what it does vs. what it will do when run" question SONNY-10 recorded and SONNY-33 reframes; it is deliberately consistent with SONNY-33's decided direction rather than a separate call.
- **`PathWhitelist`'s case-sensitive prefix comparison on a case-insensitive filesystem** is pre-existing and inherited, not introduced here — but scope matching multiplies its exposure, so SONNY-36 owes it a decision and a test rather than silence.
- **Branch C's non-relaxable floor is explicitly branch C's call**, not decided here. The finding that makes it answerable: no capability in v1 has a default tier of 3, so every tier 3 reachable today comes from an escalation, and all six escalation sites say some form of "X already exists and would be replaced." Branch C's tier-3 clause is entirely about escalated tier 3; the spec's real tier-3 capabilities (send, upload, delete, submit, purchase, share) do not exist yet.

Open questions (required, write "none" if true): none — every open item above is either a filed ticket contract or an explicitly recorded branch-C decision.

Next branch: `feature/workspace-restriction-scope` (SONNY-36 first, which is unblocked immediately; SONNY-37 onward after SONNY-29 merges). This planning branch is docs-only and merges independently.

### Branch: feature/sonny-31-unattended-escalation-handling
Status: complete
Date: 2026-08-04
Tickets: SONNY-31 — a scheduled routine that saves a snippet ran exactly once and was skipped forever after; fixed at the source (an escalation that described a change that does not happen) and in the aftermath (an eternal skip becomes a one-shot pause that says why). Spawned no discovery tickets.
Reviewed by: fresh session (per WORKFLOW.md step 7) on PR #27; eight findings, all closed before merge. **F1 was a real defect this branch introduced:** `commitScheduleDraft` rebuilds a schedule through `RoutineSchedule.newlyCreated`, which had no `pausedReason` parameter, so editing a paused routine's run time silently discarded both the caption and the reason and left the routine switched off with nothing saying why — H2's own failure state, reached through the editor door. Fixed, pinned in both directions, and mutation-checked. F3 added a view-model-level `lastRunAt` assertion the store-level test alone did not cover; F7 made the last non-hermetic `Date()` injectable; F8 counts notice emissions rather than reading the property once; F4 re-homed an orphaned doc comment. **F2 and F5 corrected two claims in this very entry that were confidently wrong** — both corrections are recorded inline below rather than quietly edited away, because CLAUDE.md sends future sessions here for exactly this kind of reasoning. F6 is deferred to SONNY-46 with the user's ratification. The reviewer then re-ran the whole mutation battery against the fixed tree and raised three more, all documentation and test-precision: **F9**, an ordering inside `newlyCreated` asserted load-bearing in three places and pinned by none — the reviewer moved the assignment and the suite stayed green — now pinned at the level it lives; **F10** and **F11**, two checkable claims inside the F2/F5 corrections that were themselves wrong (a test count that no longer reproduces, and an incomplete `tier4` enumeration), both restated. Every behavior added by this branch was mutation-checked individually against the full suite, by the implementing session and independently by the reviewer; the finding-by-finding tables are on SONNY-31's comments. **The user's manual-test pass then found two things no reviewer had:** the unattended-trust advisory still describing the behavior this branch replaced (fixed here under a one-item never-touch amendment), and that the planner has no snippet vocabulary at all, so the snippet-step routine H1's user story depends on is unauthorable through any UI path — filed as **SONNY-48**, untriaged, with the planner deliberately untouched on this branch.

Files changed:

- `Sources/MacAgentCore/RoutineSchedule.swift`

- `Sources/MacAgentCore/UnattendedTrustAdvisory.swift` (copy and doc comments only — see the never-touch amendment below)

Tests: `env CLANG_MODULE_CACHE_PATH="$PWD/.build/clang-module-cache" swift test --disable-sandbox -Xswiftc -F -Xswiftc /Library/Developer/CommandLineTools/Library/Developer/Frameworks -Xlinker -rpath -Xlinker /Library/Developer/CommandLineTools/Library/Developer/Frameworks -Xlinker -rpath -Xlinker /Library/Developer/CommandLineTools/Library/Developer/usr/lib` -> exit 0, **464 tests in 45 suites**, up from the 448 in 45 re-measured on `main` at `615d6b0` (SONNY-29 and the row-B planning PR both merged). `swift build` -> exit 0. 464 = 448 + 13 for the branch itself, + 2 for review finding F1, + 1 for review finding F9.

Behavior added:
- **H1 — re-saving a snippet with identical text no longer escalates.** `SnippetSaveCapabilityAdapter.assessRisk` escalated on the trigger merely *existing*; it now escalates only when the stored expansion differs from the step's. A saved routine re-saves the same snippet on every run, so the old condition made a scheduled snippet routine assess tier 3 from run two onward — the tier an unattended run structurally cannot satisfy.
- **H2 — a refused scheduled run pauses the schedule instead of being skipped forever.** `RoutineSchedule` gains an Optional `pausedReason` and a `pause(reason:)`; `RoutineStore.pauseSchedule(routineNamed:reason:)` writes it; `AgentViewModel`'s `RiskApprovalError` catch calls it and posts one notice naming the real cause. Re-enabling through the existing row toggle clears the reason and re-anchors the catch-up baseline.
- **The unattended-trust advisory now describes pausing rather than skipping.** Found in manual testing, and the reason this ticket's never-touch list was amended (below). Turning on unattended trust for a routine that currently assesses tier 3+ used to warn that "scheduled runs will be skipped rather than run unattended… can still be skipped later" — copy written for the behavior this branch replaced, shown at the exact moment the user is deciding whether to trust a routine to run on its own. It now says Sonny will pause the schedule and say why, in the same voice, shape and length.
- **The Routines row shows "Paused" and the routine detail view carries the reason.** The row's caption takes the slot `nextRunText` already leaves empty for any disabled schedule, in the warning colour the streak badge already uses.

Behavior preserved (required, no blanket claims):
- **The tier-3+ unattended backstop is byte-identical.** `AgentRunner` still re-assesses at execute time and still requires `approvedTier >= effectiveTier`; nothing in this branch touches it, `RiskApproval.swift`, `UnattendedTrustAdvisory.swift`, or `RoutineScheduler.swift`. The refused run in `aRoutineThatEscalatesToTierThreeIsRefusedByTheApprovalGate` is still refused — the snippet's stored text is still `"Old text"` afterwards.
- **A snippet save that really would replace different text still escalates to tier 3, with the same message.** `resavingExistingSnippetTriggerEscalatesToTierThree` passes unmodified, including its `involvedResource == "Snippet: ;sig"` assertion.
- **The user's own disable stays anonymous.** `setEnabled(false, now:)` records no reason, so a manually switched-off routine grows no "Sonny paused this" caption — pinned by `theUsersOwnDisableRecordsNoPauseReason` and by the row presentation test's user-disabled case.
- **Every existing catch-up-anchor rule is unchanged**: enabling anchors, re-enabling re-anchors, an idempotent enable leaves the baseline alone, disabling keeps it. All four `RoutineScheduleTests` cases pass unmodified, and `pause` deliberately does not touch `lastRunAt`.
- **A schedule edit no longer loses a pause** (PR #27, F1). `commitScheduleDraft` carries `pausedReason` through `newlyCreated`, which sets it before calling `setEnabled` — so the single rule about clearing a pause stays in one place: enabling clears it, staying disabled keeps it. Pinned by `editingAPausedRoutinesRunTimeKeepsItPausedWithItsReason`, and the ordering that decides the enabling half by `newlyCreatedClearsAPassedPauseReasonWhenItBuildsAnEnabledSchedule` (PR #27, F9 — that ordering was claimed load-bearing in three places and pinned by none until the re-check moved the assignment and watched the suite stay green; it is unreachable from `commitScheduleDraft`, since a schedule carrying a reason is always disabled, so it is pinned at the `RoutineSchedule` level where it lives rather than through a view-model path that cannot reach it). `editingARoutineAfterResumingItDoesNotBringItsOldPauseBack` covers resume-then-edit, the sequence a user fixing a paused routine performs; it was renamed from a name that claimed the enabling case it does not exercise.
- **Legacy `routines.json` still loads.** `pausedReason` is Optional for the same synthesized-`Decodable` reason as `schedule` and `recentRunDates`; pinned by a schedule-JSON-without-the-key decode test alongside the existing routine-JSON-without-`schedule` one.
- **One existing test's assertion was deliberately changed, and only its message.** `aRoutineThatEscalatesToTierThreeIsRefusedByTheApprovalGate` asserted the notice contained "needs your explicit approval"; that copy no longer exists because the behavior it described no longer exists. The test now asserts the pause, the persisted reason, and that the tier-3 action still did not happen — strictly more than before. No test was deleted or weakened.

Architectural decisions / pitfalls discovered (required, write "none" if true):

**"Exactly one notification" is an emergent property of pausing, not a counter — and writing it as a counter would have been the bug.** `scheduledRunNotice` is published once per attempt and `AppDelegate` mirrors it to a system notification; a disabled schedule produces no further attempts, so there is nothing left to post. A "have I already notified about this routine?" flag would have been a second source of truth for the same fact, able to drift out of sync with the schedule's actual state — and would have had to be persisted, invalidated on re-enable, and reasoned about on every other path that posts a notice.

**All three `RiskApprovalError` cases pause, not only `.approvalRequired` — because it is the smaller change, not because the other two are reachable.** PR #27's review corrected the original justification here, which claimed leaving them out would keep the bug alive "in two of the three branches that reach it". Neither of the other two is reachable today: nothing in the app ever constructs a `RiskApprovalPolicy` with `tier2Mode == .previewOnly` (`AgentViewModel.swift:467-474` already says exactly this), and no adapter anywhere produces `.tier4` — no capability declares `defaultRiskTier: .tier4`, and every `CapabilityRiskEscalation` in `MacAgentCore` targets `.tier3`. That is the claim carrying the weight; the enumeration first written here ("the only `tier4` references outside the enum declaration are two copy switches in `AgentActionExecutor`") was itself wrong, as PR #27's re-check caught. The complete list is five references, all exhaustive-`switch` arms over the tier enum and none of them a producer: `RiskApproval.swift:92` (the policy `requirement(for:)` mapping, the arm that returns `.refuse`), `:234` (`displayName`), `:249` (`defaultApprovalRule`), and `AgentActionExecutor.swift:721` and `:865` (risk-reason copy). So `.previewOnly` and `.refused` are unreachable, and the honest reason for handling all three is that the `catch` is already unified across them: pausing on one would have meant *splitting* the block and writing a second, divergent outcome for two branches nobody can currently reach. Both are equally permanent for a run with nobody present to approve anything, so if either ever does become reachable, pausing is the behavior that was already correct.

**The pause write must happen inside the store, not from a caller-held copy — and the reason is the suspension point, not a retry loop.** `RoutineStore.pauseSchedule` does its own read-modify-write, exactly like `advanceScheduleBaseline` beside it. The rule it obeys is the one `RoutineStore`'s own type comment states: *never read a routine, `await` anything, then write it back* — a read-modify-write spanning a suspension point can interleave with another main-actor task and silently drop the loser's write, and the scheduler's timer is this codebase's first background writer, so two writers genuinely can want the same record. In `performScheduledRun` the only place to take that copy is before `try await runner.execute`, and the write-back lands after it; `resolveOccurrence`'s advance of `schedule.lastRunAt` is one of the writes that would be reverted, and any concurrent user edit during the await is another.

**PR #27's review corrected this entry's original causal claim, and the correction is worth keeping visible** because a future session reading only the changelog would otherwise inherit a false model of the scheduler. The first version of this paragraph said a reverted `lastRunAt` would put the occurrence back in play and resurrect the forever-retry. That is empirically false, and the refutation is stated here as history rather than as something to re-run, because re-running it now gives the opposite result: at `cc1d733`, before F3 existed, the reviewer implemented the caller-held write-back and the full suite **passed 461/461** — nothing anywhere observed the reverted baseline, because `RoutineScheduler.decision` returns `.notDue` for any disabled schedule (`RoutineScheduler.swift:46-48`) and every re-enable re-anchors the baseline anyway. A paused schedule cannot retry regardless of what its baseline says. **F3's assertion then closed exactly that hole**: at `7354395` the same mutation reddens `aRoutineThatEscalatesToTierThreeIsRefusedByTheApprovalGate`, so a session that tries it today gets a red suite from the fix, not a green one from the bug. Both facts belong together — the original claim was wrong about *why* the write must happen in the store, and the property is now pinned anyway. The store-boundary rule above is the real, and stronger, justification — it is a documented invariant of the type rather than a downstream symptom.

**H1's comparison has to be against the *trimmed* expansion.** `snippetSpec` trims the step's text and `SnippetStore.save` stores the trimmed value, so comparing raw step text against a stored value would report a change for any saved routine whose snippet step happens to end in a newline — re-opening the identical hole for a subset of routines, in a way no obvious test would catch. `updatedAt` is deliberately excluded from the comparison: it moves on every save by design, so including it would mean nothing is ever identical.

**Never-touch list amended by the user, by exactly one item, for a reason worth generalizing.** `UnattendedTrustAdvisory.swift` was on this ticket's never-touch list — correctly, because its *semantics* are branch 10's structural backstop and nothing here should have touched them. What the list did not anticipate is that the file also holds user-facing **copy describing the very behavior this branch changes**. Manual testing found the advisory still promising the pre-pause behavior, which meant the app was contradicting itself at the moment of highest stakes: the user deciding whether to let a routine run unattended. The user amended the list by that one file, for copy only; everything else on it (`AgentRunner.swift`, the tier definitions and `RiskApprovalPolicy` mapping, `RoutineScheduler.swift`'s date math) stands, and the advisory's gating logic here is byte-identical — same tier-3 threshold, same `try?` assessment, same nil results.

**The generalizable lesson: a never-touch entry protects a file's *behavior*, but copy inside that file can still go stale when the behavior it narrates lives elsewhere.** Three doc comments in the same file were stale for the same reason and are corrected here too, since CLAUDE.md sends future sessions to comments before code. One of them was stale because of *this branch's own H1*: the best-effort paragraph cited "a snippet save that would replace an existing trigger" as an escalation example, which after H1 is only true when the text differs. Nothing else in the repo caught that — a reminder to grep for prose describing a condition whenever the condition itself is narrowed.

**Wireframe-fidelity exception, stated rather than silent (per CLAUDE.md's rule).** `11-MainAppRoutines.svg` specifies the row's right-hand slot as a next-run caption and specifies no paused state at all, because the wireframe predates a schedule that can pause itself. The exception is bounded to the smallest possible shape: the caption occupies a slot that is *already empty* in this state (`RoutineScheduleDisplay.nextRunText` returns nil for any disabled schedule), uses the row's existing warning token rather than a new one, and adds no line to a 56pt row with space for neither. The reason sentence lives in the routine detail view — a surface the ticket names, with room for prose. Pinned by asserting `nextRunText == nil` for the paused routine, so a future change that gives a disabled schedule a next-run caption collides with this test rather than silently stacking two captions in one slot.

Known limitations / deferred scope:
- **The general changed-content collision class remains module C input, unchanged.** H1 fixes the deterministic case — a routine re-saving byte-identical snippet text. A routine whose `.createLocalDraft`/`.createZip`/`.writeMarkdown` step carries an explicit non-timestamped `outputPath` still escalates on every run after the first, correctly (the file really does exist and really would be overwritten), and now pauses instead of skipping forever. That is the intended outcome for this branch, not an oversight: whether a routine should be allowed to overwrite its own prior output unattended is the product question module C owns.
- **The system notification still respects the pre-existing surface-visible gate.** `AppDelegate` posts a notification for `scheduledRunNotice` only when no Sonny surface is on screen. "Exactly one notification" therefore means *at most* one; with Command Center open the user gets the in-app notice instead. That gate predates this branch and is deliberately left as found.
- **Deferred to SONNY-46, ratified by the user 2026-08-05 (PR #27, F6): the `pausedReason != nil` ⇒ `!isEnabled` invariant is documentation-enforced, not type-enforced.** `pause(reason:)` is the only writer and it always disables, `setEnabled(true,)` always clears, and the field's own doc comment states the implication — but nothing stops a future caller assigning `pausedReason` directly on an enabled schedule, which would render a "Paused" caption on a routine that is merrily still running (the row checks `isPaused` before `nextRunText`, so it would also suppress the real caption). Making it structural — a private setter, or folding both fields into one enum so the illegal state is unrepresentable — is a public-API change to a `Codable` type with a persisted, encrypted on-disk representation, so it needs its own migration story and a decision between shapes. That is more than this ticket's scope. **Deferred deliberately rather than silently: the user ratified it and SONNY-46 (Backlog, untriaged) is its named landing spot**, per CLAUDE.md's fix-in-branch rule, which requires both. SONNY-46 also records the one-line presentation-layer hardening that would remove the visible symptom independently of whichever model shape is chosen. **Closed 2026-08-06 by `feature/automation-store-hardening`** — the enum fold, not the private setter; see that entry below.
- **H1's motivating user story is not reachable through the UI today, and that is a separate gap (SONNY-48).** The planner declares no tool for either snippet operation (`plannerTools: []` on both adapters), so it cannot author a routine containing a `save_snippet` step, while `SaveRoutineCapabilityAdapter.validateRoutineSteps` permits exactly that shape — the permissive allowlist is what this ticket's contract was reasonably written against. H1's behavior change is still live and user-facing on the *direct* path (`InstantCommandResolver` resolves "snippet save …" without the planner), and the scheduled composition is pinned by tests at the core layer where it is constructible; what cannot currently be reached is that escalation arriving inside a scheduled routine. H2 is manually verified through the general collision class instead — a `create_local_draft` routine with an explicit output path colliding with its own first run, which is fully authorable and exercises the entire pause path.
- **A paused routine is discoverable only from the Routines list.** There is no aggregate "some routines need attention" indicator anywhere else in the app. Adding one would be a new UI surface, which the ticket explicitly rules out.

Open questions (required, write "none" if true): none.

Next branch: SONNY-24, the last ticket of this session's pre-assigned serial sequence (SONNY-29 → SONNY-31 → SONNY-24), on its own branch after this one merges — all three touch the executor/assessment area, so the sequence is serial through merge.

### Branch: feature/sonny-24-routine-browser-binding
Status: complete
Date: 2026-08-05
Tickets: SONNY-24 — a routine's URL steps now open in the routine's own browser, the way a workspace's URLs already did. Spawned no discovery tickets.
Reviewed by: fresh session (per WORKFLOW.md step 7) on PR #28; eight findings, all closed before merge. Two were unpinned edits this branch shipped — the Hacker News adapter's read of `preferredBrowser` (F2, revertible with the suite green) and the unresolvable-app skip branch (F3) — both now pinned and mutation-checked. Two were false claims in this entry and in the code comments: "every URL the routine opens" ignored the media seam (F4), and "structurally moot" ignored that `RoutineStore.save` does not validate steps (F5). The rest were a mutation-table count measured before later tests existed (F1), a comment claiming a fixture excluded an implementation it does not (F6), a leaked temp directory (F7), and a founder-decision reference the repository could not resolve (F8).

**Mutation counts are stated with the commit they were measured at, from this branch onward.** The table on SONNY-24 originally recorded the binding-revert mutation as failing 4 tests, measured at `1e5f13c`; the reviewer measured 5 at `ddf0da5`; it is 7 at `ead2c42`. Nothing regressed — each new pin adds a test that the same revert reddens. A count with no SHA beside it cannot be told apart from a stale one, which is the whole reason F1 was raisable.

Spec sections covered: §6.9/§9.2-§9.3 territory (workspaces and routines as saved automations) — the routine half of the browser-targeting behavior SONNY-9 built for workspaces. No risk tier, approval mapping, or planner vocabulary changed.
Files changed:
- `Sources/MacAgentCore/CapabilityAdapter.swift` (`preferredBrowser` on the execution context, `ExecuteNestedPlan` signature)
- `Sources/MacAgentCore/AgentActionExecutor.swift` (threading)
- `Sources/MacAgentCore/RunRoutineCapabilityAdapter.swift` (browser resolution)
- `Sources/MacAgentCore/OpenSafeURLCapabilityAdapter.swift`
- `Sources/MacAgentCore/OpenAppSearchURLCapabilityAdapter.swift`
- `Sources/MacAgentCore/WebResearchMarkdownCapabilityAdapter.swift`
- `Tests/MacAgentCoreTests/AgentActionExecutorTests.swift`
- `docs/sonny-v1-implementation-changelog.md`

Tests: `env CLANG_MODULE_CACHE_PATH="$PWD/.build/clang-module-cache" swift test --disable-sandbox -Xswiftc -F -Xswiftc /Library/Developer/CommandLineTools/Library/Developer/Frameworks -Xlinker -rpath -Xlinker /Library/Developer/CommandLineTools/Library/Developer/Frameworks -Xlinker -rpath -Xlinker /Library/Developer/CommandLineTools/Library/Developer/usr/lib` -> exit 0, **471 tests in 45 suites**, up from the 464 in 45 re-measured on `main` at `0a33a03` (post-SONNY-31 merge). `swift build` -> exit 0.

Behavior added:
- **A routine's URL steps open in the routine's own browser.** `RunRoutineCapabilityAdapter` resolves the first browser-capable app among the routine's own `.openApp` steps and passes it into the nested execution; every URL that plan opens *on the injected browser-opener seam* goes there. "Open Safari → open github.com" now lands in Safari rather than the system default.
- **Order-independent, first-wins**, per the founder decision of 2026-08-04 — the first browser-capable app anywhere in the routine binds all of its URL steps regardless of order; order-sensitive binding and a per-routine browser setting were both declined so a routine and a workspace stay one mental model. A URL step sequenced *before* the browser step still binds, and with two browsers the first in step order wins.
- **The binding covers every URL the routine opens *on the injected browser-opener seam*** — `.openURL`, `.openAppSearchURL` and the Hacker News open, since all three read the same context field. **Not `.playMedia`**, which opens through `context.mediaOpener` and reaches `NSWorkspace.shared.open` directly (`MediaPlaybackService.swift:1015`), so a media step carrying an explicit `open.spotify.com`-style URL still lands in the system default browser. The unqualified "every URL the routine opens" was this entry's original claim and was too broad (PR #28, F4); the gap is filed as its own ticket.

Behavior preserved (required, no blanket claims):
- **The ordinary open-URL path is untouched, and this is the branch's central guard.** A plain "open github.com", a standalone app-search URL, and the Hacker News open all still go to the system default. Six pre-existing `openedBrowsers == [nil]` assertions (added by SONNY-9's F1 precisely to make this provable) stay `[nil]`, and each was re-proved live by forcing a browser into its own adapter: `OpenSafeURLCapabilityAdapter` → 2 red, `OpenAppSearchURLCapabilityAdapter` → 2 red, the Hacker News open → 1 red.
- **The workspace path is byte-identical.** `OpenWorkspaceCapabilityAdapter` still resolves and passes its own browser explicitly; it never reads `preferredBrowser`. Its tests pass unmodified.
- **A routine naming no browser is unchanged** — resolves `nil`, system default, pinned by `aRoutineWithNoBrowserStepStillUsesTheSystemDefault`.
- **`WorkspaceBrowserOpener`'s fallback semantics are inherited, not forked.** An unresolvable or uninstalled browser still falls back to the default and logs; nothing in this branch touches that contract or `SafeURL` validation.
- **Scheduled and manual runs behave identically**, pinned through `AgentRunner` at `.approved(.tier2)` — the only tier an unattended run can carry.

Architectural decisions / pitfalls discovered (required, write "none" if true):

**The browser is a parameter, never executor state — and the reason generalizes to anything else threaded through `execute`.** The tempting shortcut is a `private var routineBrowser` on `AgentActionExecutor`, set around the nested call and restored in a `defer`. It is wrong for the same reason SONNY-31's caller-held schedule write was: `execute` is `async` and suspends at every step, so executor-held state is readable *and mutable* by any other main-actor task that interleaves — a scheduled routine's browser could leak into a command the user typed meanwhile, and the `defer` would restore a value the other task had already moved on from. Threading it costs 24 mechanical switch-arm edits and buys correctness by construction. **`@MainActor` serializes individual synchronous calls, not the span of an `async` function.**

**Reuse of `WorkspaceBrowserCatalog.firstBrowser(in:)` is the point, not an implementation convenience.** Two definitions of "browser-capable" would drift the moment one gains Arc and the other does not, and the user-visible promise of this ticket is that a routine and a workspace behave the same way. The catalog's own doc comment already anticipated this ("adding one to the catalog makes it browser-aware in the same edit").

**Unresolvable app names are skipped during resolution, deliberately.** `compactMap { try? … }` rather than `try`: a routine step naming an app the catalog no longer knows must fail when *that step* runs, with its own error, not be pre-empted by a different failure raised before any step has executed. Fail-fast here would also have been a behavior change beyond this ticket, since routines dispatch per step today.

**One open question from the discovery note is unhandled rather than impossible, and the difference is a store-boundary asymmetry worth knowing.** It asked whether a nested workspace-open inside a routine should keep winning with its own browser. `SaveRoutineCapabilityAdapter.validateRoutineSteps` rejects `.openWorkspace` (and `.runRoutine`) — but that is the *save capability's* boundary, not the store's. `RoutineStore.save` validates `schedule` and nothing else, so a routine carrying either can be written straight to the store and reach the executor; 52 call-site lines across 48 test functions in 11 files already write routines that way (measured by PR #28's reviewer at `b31d7e2`; this entry's original "nineteen" came from a single narrow grep and was simply wrong), and this branch's own skip-branch test needs that door to exist. This entry originally called the question "structurally moot", which was wrong (PR #28, F5). The asymmetry — `RoutineSchedule.validate()` is enforced at the store's single choke point while step safety is enforced only one layer up — is filed as its own ticket. **Correction appended 2026-08-06 by `feature/automation-store-hardening` (PR #30 review, R2), additive — nothing above is rewritten. Two claims in this paragraph no longer hold.** The "52 call-site lines across 48 test functions in 11 files" figure predates that branch and is itself an undercount: it was measured by grep, which cannot type-resolve a receiver named `store` and misses dotted receivers such as `fixture.routineStore.save(`. The compiler-verified population at `ca4fbe4` is **74 call-site lines across 57 enclosing functions in 12 files**, reproduced independently by PR #30's reviewer. And "this branch's own skip-branch test needs that door to exist" is no longer true as written now that SONNY-52 validates steps at `RoutineStore.save`: that test's routine is three steps of `.openApp`/`.openURL`/`.openApp`, all legal operations, so what it needs is a *direct-store* door, not an *unchecked-steps* one — the save capability refuses it through `previewNestedPlan` on an unresolvable app name, which no store can evaluate. It still calls plain `save`, and correctly. See this file's `feature/automation-store-hardening` entry for the sweep method and the two sites that did need the sanctioned bypass.

**The SONNY-9 review chain's assertion-flip prediction was exact, and it is the model for how to hand a scope guard forward.** That chain added six `[nil]` assertions *before* this ticket existed, having first proved by mutation that nothing pinned the "before" state. This branch's first test run then flipped exactly one of them — the routine site — with the other five green, which is precisely the signal the chain said would distinguish a correct fix from one leaking into the ordinary open-URL path. A ticket that pins the before-state for its own successor is worth copying.

Known limitations / deferred scope:
- **No per-routine browser setting** — option (c) at triage, declined. The browser is inferred from the routine's own steps, so a routine that opens no browser cannot be made to prefer one without adding an `.openApp` step.
- **`.switchRunningApp` steps do not contribute a browser.** Only `.openApp` steps are scanned. Switching to an already-running Safari is arguably the same intent, but the ticket's wording is "app-open step" and widening it was not contracted; recorded rather than silently decided.
- **A routine's browser does not reach a nested plan that a *capability* runs on its own** — there is no such path today (routines cannot nest), so this is a note for whoever adds one.
- **`.playMedia` URL steps do not bind (SONNY-51).** They open on the media seam rather than the browser seam, so an explicit `open.spotify.com`-style URL in a routine still uses the system default. Filed rather than fixed: it needs the media seam to accept a browser preference, which is a different seam than this ticket contracted for.
- **`RoutineStore.save` validates schedules but not steps (SONNY-52).** A routine the save capability rejects can still be written directly to the store and executed. Filed rather than fixed: tightening it would change what 52 call-site lines across 48 test functions in 11 files are allowed to write, which is more than a browser-binding ticket should decide. **Closed 2026-08-06 by `feature/automation-store-hardening`** — and the "52 / 48 / 11" figure quoted here and at F5 above was itself an undercount, measured by grep; the compiler-verified population is 74 call-site lines in 57 enclosing functions across 12 files, of which exactly two needed the sanctioned bypass. See that entry below for the method.
- **The routine-browser-binding decision is not yet in `docs/sonny-founder-design-decisions.md` (SONNY-53).** The decision is recorded on SONNY-24 and spelled out in `RunRoutineCapabilityAdapter`'s doc comment, but that document is where founder decisions are meant to live, and it currently has no entry for this one.

Open questions (required, write "none" if true): none.

Next branch: none. This closes the pre-assigned serial sequence SONNY-29 → SONNY-31 → SONNY-24; roadmap row B (`feature/workspace-restriction-scope`, SONNY-36 onward) is planned and unstarted.

Four record-only residuals, none needing a fix round: three closed in repo text at `a906c93` — step 7's late-failure bullet now names the actor making the fix commits, "Who does what" gains the coordinating-session role definition, and this entry's misnumbered self-reference now names amendment 7 — and the fourth comment-side only: the closing comment's divergent "six amended clauses" enumeration, resolved by ratifying the changelog's set as the record. Spec sections covered: none, and this is the only branch so far where that is the honest answer — nothing here touches product behavior, the risk model, or any spec section. `docs/sonny-major-release-spec.md` is untouched.
Files changed:
- `WORKFLOW.md` (steps 2, 3, 4, 5, 7)
- `CLAUDE.md` (new `## Claims and evidence` section; two Conventions bullets amended)
- `docs/sonny-founder-design-decisions.md` (Routines section)
- `docs/sonny-v1-implementation-changelog.md`

Tests: **not run, deliberately** — the diff is confined to two root Markdown files and two files under `docs/`, which is exactly the case step 7 exempts, and no `Sources/` or `Tests/` file exists in it (`git diff --stat ca4fbe4..58a1f92` lists four `.md` paths and nothing else, this entry's own commit included). The suite's last measurement stands unchanged and is quoted rather than re-asserted: 471 tests in 45 suites, the figure the SONNY-24 entry above records for `feature/sonny-24-routine-browser-binding`, merged as `ca4fbe4`. This branch adds no code for it to move, and it was not re-measured here. Verification here is the read-through pass, a key-phrase sweep, and the adversarial audit above — all recorded on SONNY-57's closing comment with their evidence.

Behavior added (process, not product):
- **Eight rules in `WORKFLOW.md`**, each at the step it governs: the reviewer's step-0 head-SHA confirm and the stop-if-it-moved rule; the three-round review cap (initial → one fix round → one re-check); interim reviews scaled to what the ticket touched (behavior-touching → full treatment, docs/strings-only → light pass); reviewers never implementing, committing or pushing, with fix prompts naming their target session (all step 7); the role-split worktree lifecycle, where an implementing session's worktree spans its whole assigned sequence and a reviewer never removes its own (step 3); never predicting a ticket number instead of reading back the returned `sequence_id` (step 2); never-touch exceptions as intent-over-letter questions requiring explicit user ratification with narrow discharge conditions *before* the file is touched (step 5); and the launching prompt being context while the pulled description is the contract (step 4).
- **Three rules in `CLAUDE.md`** under a new `## Claims and evidence` section: enumerate before you subtract; a quantified claim is checked against the whole population with a markup-tolerant sweep; every reported measurement carries the SHA it was measured at.
- **The routine-browser decision is now findable where CLAUDE.md sends a session looking for founder decisions** — the rule, both declined alternatives (order-sensitive binding; an explicit per-routine browser setting) with the single reason they were declined, and the seam caveat that the binding reaches the injected browser-opener seam and not `.playMedia` (SONNY-51).

Behavior preserved (required, no blanket claims) — for a docs branch this means the existing clauses the new rules touched, each named and each still saying what it said:
- **The suite-rerun exemption is unchanged and was not widened.** It still reads "a diff provably confined to docs/comments may skip the rerun — anything touching `Sources/` or `Tests/` never skips, and no session invents its own threshold beyond that line." Amendment 3's light pass is a *depth* setting layered above it, and says so in its own text: a strings-only diff still touches `Sources/` and still reruns.
- **The fix-in-branch rule is unchanged, and the review cap says so explicitly.** The cap bounds rounds of review, not fixes; a defect found at any point is still fixed in the branch before merge.
- **The trivial fast path is unchanged** — still user-classified only, still keeps the full contract, the evidence requirement and the user-merge gate. Amendment 3 sits beside it as a different mechanism (a fresh session reviewing less deeply, versus no reviewer session at all).
- **The standing commit/push authorization still exists in full** for the session implementing a ticket; it is now scoped to that session rather than to "sessions", and step 3's `--force-with-lease` rebase clause it references is untouched.
- **Step 2's ticket-content contract, step 4's reconcile-before-implementing rule, and step 5's discovered-work and stop-and-report clauses all still stand**; the three new rules landing next to them (9, 11, 10 respectively) extend those clauses rather than replacing any of them.
- **`docs/sonny-founder-design-decisions.md`'s existing Routines entries are byte-identical** — the new decision is appended after them, and the document's authority statement (authoritative over a naive reading of the wireframes) is untouched.

Architectural decisions / pitfalls discovered (required, write "none" if true):

**The split between the two files is "does this rule exist outside a ticket's lifecycle?", not "is it important?"** Amendments 4, 5 and 6 govern every claim made in this repo — chat, code comments, ticket comments, changelog prose — and `CLAUDE.md` is the always-loaded file, so they went there. The other eight are steps in the ticket lifecycle and would be inert outside it. The seam between the two files is a single pointer: step 5's "Evidence, not assertion" bullet points at the `CLAUDE.md` section without restating it, which is what keeps the no-duplication criterion true rather than nearly true.

**Codifying a rule mostly means finding the clause it silently contradicts.** Six existing clauses had to be amended in place, and three of them would otherwise have been live contradictions on the day this merged: step 3 said "one worktree per ticket" while amendment 7 makes an implementer worktree span the session's whole sequence; step 5's standing authorization said "sessions commit and push" while amendment 8 forbids exactly that for reviewers; and step 7's post-merge close-out ordered the worktree removed after *this* branch merges while amendment 7 keeps it alive for the session's next ticket. Two more came from `CLAUDE.md`'s condensed restatement of the review — "reruns the real test suite" and an unscoped commit authorization — which is the structural cost of a summary living in a second file: **every rule change has to be checked against the summary, not only against the source of truth.** The sixth was step 7's findings-routing sentence, which read as the reviewer authoring the fix commits.

**A line-based `grep` is the wrong instrument for verifying rules in these files, and this branch proved it on itself.** The acceptance criterion was "each rule's key phrase appears exactly once across `WORKFLOW.md` + `CLAUDE.md`". The first sweep reported *zero* for the reviewer-worktree rule — not because it was missing, but because `WORKFLOW.md` wraps at ~90 columns and the phrase straddled a line break. The passing sweep strips `*`/`_`/backticks and collapses whitespace before counting. That is amendment 5's rule catching a defect in the verification of amendment 7, on its first run — the under-counted phrase was the reviewer-worktree rule's.

**Dates in this repo are local, and Plane's are UTC.** SONNY-57's own description dates the review cap 2026-08-06 because Plane stamped the triage session at `2026-08-06T02:28:47Z` — 22:28 on 2026-08-05 in the machine's zone. Every date in the changelog and the founder-decisions doc is local, so the UTC date would have read as a future one against everything around it. The amendment is written 2026-08-05, with the deviation from the contract's literal wording recorded on the ticket rather than made silently. Worth knowing generally: a ticket's `created_at` is not the date to copy into a document.

Known limitations / deferred scope:
- **Three of the eleven trigger incidents are unverifiable from this repository, by nature.** The PR #26 reviewer/implementer race, the two mis-pasted fix prompts, and SONNY-56's "tells them nothing" live in Plane comments and terminal history, not in commits — the adversarial audit confirmed the other incidents' artifacts (SONNY-44's "act log" and "both surfaces" commits, SONNY-24's 52/48/11 numbers, PR #28's F1) and correctly declined to flag these three. A future reader gets the rule and the incident's name, not a diff to open.
- **Nothing here is machine-enforced.** These are rules a session reads, with no lint, hook, or CI check behind any of them; the enforcement is that `CLAUDE.md` is always loaded and `WORKFLOW.md` is read at ticket start. Building enforcement was not in scope and would be its own decision.
- **Amendment 3 introduces interim reviews to `WORKFLOW.md` for the first time.** They were already happening in practice on row B; the document previously described review only at PR time. This entry records that as a real addition to the process, not a clarification of it.
- **`docs/sonny-manual-test-checklist.md` was not touched.** No manual-test item exists for either ticket.

Open questions (required, write "none" if true): none.
